Output:  The problem asks to differentiate the function \(f(x) = x^4 - 2x^2 + 1\) with respect to \(x\). Differentiation finds the rate of change, and we use the power rule for each term.

Step 1: Apply the power rule \(\frac{d}{dx}[x^n] = n x^{n-1}\) to the first term.
\[\frac{d}{dx}(x^4) = 4x^3.\]

Step 2: Differentiate the second term, handling the constant coefficient by multiplication.
\[f(x) \text{ has a term } -2x^2. \quad \frac{d}{dx}(-2x^2) = -2 \cdot 2x^{1} = -4x.\]

Step 3: Differentiate the third term (a constant).
\[\frac{d}{dx}(1) = 0.\]

Combine these results:
\[f'(x) = 4x^3 + (-4x) + 0 = 4x^3 - 4x.\]

The final answer is \(f'(x) = 4x^3 - 4x\).

We see that both numerator and denominator have the factor `(x - 1)`. We can cancel this common factor, *but only if x ≠ 1* because division by zero is undefined.
After cancelling the `(x - 1)` terms, we are left with: `x + 1`

This simplified expression works for all values of x except x = 1.

Here's the problem: First expand the expression (x + 1)**2, and then factor it completely.

*(Step 4):* Here, a = x and b = 1.
*(Step 5):* Therefore, the factored form is (x + 1) times (x + 1).

So:
The final answer after both steps is \( (x + 1)(x + 1) \).

Output:  The problem asks you to:
1.  Expand \((x + 1)^2\) by multiplying it out using FOIL or another method.
2.  Take that expanded expression and factor it back into a product of factors.

Here's how I do both steps:

**Step-by-Step Working:**

*   **Expansion:** Start with the original expression, \((x + 1)^2\). This means multiplying \((x + 1)\) by itself. Using FOIL (First, Outer, Inner, Last):
    *   First: \(x \cdot x = x^2\)
    *   Outer: \(x \cdot 1 = x\) (and there's another outer term from the second factor): also \(x \cdot 1 = x\), so this contributes another \(x\).
    *   Inner: \(1 \cdot x = x\) (from the first factor and the second’s inner part).
    *   Last: \(1 \cdot 1 = 1\)
    Adding these together, we get \(x^2 + (x + x) + 1 = x^2 + 2x + 1\).

*   **Factoring:** Now, take the expanded expression, \(x^2 + 2x + 1\). Factoring means finding two factors that multiply to this result. Notice that each term has an 'x' part and a constant (or coefficient) part:
    *   The first term is x squared.
    *   The second term is two times x.
    *   The third term is one squared (1).
    This pattern often corresponds to squaring a binomial, specifically \((something + something else)^2\). Since we know the expansion came from \((x + 1)^2\), and factoring reverses that process, the factors should be identical.

**Final Answer:**

The final answer is:
\[ (x + 1)^2 \]

Output:  Okay, here's how to find and simplify that integral:

We are asked to integrate the product of sin(x) and cos(x). To do this, we use a trigonometric identity because integrating just these two functions separately isn't straightforward. The key identity is sin(2θ) = 2sin(θ)cos(θ). Rearranging gives: sin(θ)cos(θ) = (1/2) * sin(2θ).

Now, we substitute this into our integral using the rule u = 2x and du/dx = 2. This changes dx to (du)/2.

The exact result of each step is:

∫ sin(x)cos(x) dx = ∫ (1/2)*sin(2x) dx

= (1/2) * ∫ sin(u) * (du/2)   [after substitution]

= (1/4) * ∫ sin(u) du

The exact result of integrating sin(u) is -cos(u).

Substituting back u = 2x gives:

= (-1/4) cos(2x)

This method relies on recognizing the identity and performing a substitution. Since we're looking for an antiderivative, it's also correct to check that differentiating this result should give us sin(x)cos(x). But since the problem just asks for finding and simplifying:

The final answer is (-1/4) cos(2x).

Output:  Okay, let's tackle the integral of sin(x) times cos(x).

The problem asks: "Find the antiderivative (or indefinite integral) of the product of sine and cosine functions with the same argument 'x', then simplify that result."

To find this integral, we can use a substitution method or recognize it as related to a trigonometric identity. Let's use substitution.

Let **u = sin(x)**. Then the derivative is **du/dx = cos(x)**, so **du = cos(x) dx** (and also **dx = du / cos(x)**).

We substitute into the integral:

∫sin(x) * cos(x) dx  => ∫ u * cos(x) * (du / cos(x)) 

The cos(x) terms cancel out.

This simplifies to:

∫ sin(x) * cos(x) dx = ∫ u du

Now, integrate **u** with respect to **u**: (∫ u du) = (1/2) u² + C

Substitute back using the original definition of **u**:

(1/2)(sin(x))² + C

We can also recognize that sin(x)cos(x) is part of a trigonometric identity: 2 * sin(x)cos(x) = sin(2x). Therefore, sin(x)cos(x) equals (1/2) sin(2x).

So the integral becomes:

∫sin(x) cos(x) dx = ∫(1/2) sin(2x) dx

Now integrate (1/2) * sin(2x):

= (1/2) * (-1/2) * cos(2x) + C   [because d/dx(sin(2x)) = 2cos(2x), so ∫sin(2x)dx = - (1/2)cos(2x)]

But that gives: - (1/4) cos(2x) + C, which is slightly different from the previous result. Let me check my math.

If u=sin(x), du=cos(x)dx, then ∫sin(x)cos(x)dx = ∫u du = (1/2)u² + C = (1/2)(sin(x))² + C

Using identity: sin(2x)=2sin(x)cos(x), so cos(x)sin(x)=(1/2)sin(2x). Then:

∫sin(x)cos(x)dx = ∫(1/2)sin(2x)dx = (1/2)*∫sin(2x)dx

Now, integrating sin(2x): let v=2x, dv=2dx => dx=dv/2. So ∫sin(2x)dx becomes (1/2)∫sin(v)dv.

But I don't need to go through that substitution again because the identity gives:

∫sin(x)cos(x)dx = ∫(1/2)sin(2x)dx

And integrating sin(2x): the antiderivative is - (1/2) cos(2x). So we have:

= (1/2) * [ - (1/2) cos(2x) ] + C   => ∫(1/2)sin(2x)dx = (1/2)*(-cos(2x)/2) + C
